1697. MARY SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of December, 2 flannel shirts, value 7 s.; 2 pinafores, value 1 s.; 3 shirts, value 12 s.; 3 frocks, value 3 s.; 6 napkins, value 3 s.; 2 gowns, value 5 s.; 1 sheet, value 6 s.; 1 pair of stockings, value 1 s.; and 1 yard of linen cloth, value 8 d.; the goods of Peter Haslip, her master.
MARTHA HASLIP . I am the wife of Peter Haslip, of Magdalen-street, Borough. The prisoner was in my service for twelve months—on the 2nd of May my daughter wanted a frock—the prisoner said it was at the washerwoman's—we sent for it, and the woman said it was not there—the prisoner then said she had pledged it—I then asked her about a great many other things—she denied that she had pledged any thing else—I never permitted her to pledge any thing.
JOSEPH NUNWICK ROSIER . I am a pawnbroker. I produce two flannel shirts, three frocks, and the other things stated—I took part of them in of Ann Staines.
ANN STAINES . I am married. I have known the prisoner some time—she brought these things, and asked me to pawn them, which I did, in my own name—I gave her the money.
GUILTY . Aged 34.— Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18390513-1698]
1698. JOHN CALVERT was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of May 1 box, value 1 s.; and 14 medals, value 9 l.; the-goods of Thomas Hockings, in his dwelling-house.
JANE HOCKINGS . I am the wife of Thomas Hockings. We keep a coffee-shop in Black man-street—on the evening of the 2nd of May I was called down into the shop—the prisoner was standing there, in great confusion, having just let these medals fall from the box, which was kept in the window, covered with glass—there was a curtain to the window—none of my customers had any business at the window—the medals are worth 9 l.—they are silver gilt and metal—I have one here worth 1 l.—that is silver—they were there for sale—they are called medals, and are worn as such—he had only one cup of coffee, which he had partly drank, and paid for—I had never seen him before.
CHARLES WARD . I am a shoemaker, and live in Rose-street, Long acre. On the evening of the 2nd of May I went to Mr. Hockings coffee-shop—I looked in at the window about nine o'clock, and saw the Box of Temperance medals—being a member of the Society, I looked In at the window—there is to be a grand procession on Whit-Monday, and we intend wearing them—they were placed in a box, with a glass, over it and a bill on it, stating that the medals were from 4 d. to a guinea each—some were 24 s.—I went into the shop, and saw the prisoner sitting in a settle next to the window—there was nobody else there—I sat facing him about a quarter after nine o'clock I saw him standing up, and leaning over the settle, with his arm in the window—he brought the box out of the window—the hinge fell back, and he dropped the box in his confusion, and the medals were thrown out—when I came to look at the window, I found he had taken off the glass which had covered them, and put it against the
settle—I gave an alarm—the prosecutrix soon came down, and asked what he had done with the glass—he said he did not know—I stood at the door to prevent his going out—he did not attempt to go out, but seemed confused—I should say he did not know I could see him—I was perhaps three yards from him, but not in the same settle—he might have taken them without my seeing him, if I had not glanced my eye there at the moment.
Prisoner. I took the glass off the box, placed it on the settle, and was replacing the box in the window, when some of them fell out—he came and caught hold of me—he was sitting with his back to me, and says he saw me take the box out of the window—I had merely taken it out to look at them, and was replacing it at the time. Witness. On my oath he was taking it from the window, not putting it back—I did not collar him—he was not attempting to carry it away—he was merely in a confused state—he had not concealed them.
JOHN AGATE . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner, and the box of medals was carried to the station-house—I have only one here—there were three round ones like five-shilling-pieces—I went to the prisoner's mother in Union-street, Newington-causeway, and found her a very respectable woman.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390513-1699]
1699. PURCILL FORD was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of May, 6 cups, value 10 s.; 1 glass tumbler, value 1 s.; 1 brush, value 1 s.; 6 plates, value 1 l.; 2 candlesticks, value 4 s.; 1 looking-glass and frame, value 7 s.; the cover of a cushion, value 10 s.; and 1 bag, value 6 d.; the goods of George Williams and others: and 8 gas pillars, value 2 l., the the said goods of George Williams and others, fixed in a building there.
JOHN HOPKINSON . I live in Bridge-house-place, Newington-causeway—I am one of the deacons of Great Suffolk-street Baptist chapel. On the 4th of May I went there, and found Marchant, the pew-opener there, and eight or ten of the gas-fittings were taken away—I missed the articles stated, several of the lockers were broken open, the hymn books and tracts scattered about, and a box in the library up-stairs, broken open, and half-pence and farthings contributed by the children of the Sunday-school to to the Missionary Society taken out—the property belongs to the trustees and deacons, George Williams and others—they have the care and charge of them—the chapel-keeper, who is their servant, has charge of the chapel.
JOHN WARD . I am a leather-dresser, and live in Friar-street—I work in premises adjoining the chapel. On the 4th of May, at half-past five o'clock in the morning I observed some persons come out of the chapel—I watched them into Suffolk-street—I went to the door, and saw two men standing opposite Chapel-place—one gave a whistle—I called over to Brown—he made a stop opposite the door, and said, "Why don't you come out, why stand peeping there?"—the prisoner immediately came out of the chapel, and I held him while Brown ran after the other two—he said, "Do you mean to let me down?"—I said, "No"—he said, "If you do not, I will down you"—the policeman came up and took him.
JOHN BEOWN . I am a fire-wood cutter, and live in Chapel-place. I saw some men come out of the chapel about half-past five o'clock in the morning—two went over to Mr. Knight's and stood by his railings—Ward called me—I heard a whistle given, when the policeman got out of sight—I observed the chapel-door opened when the whistle was given, and the prisoner looked out, peeping at me—I said, "You have no business there,
why not come out?"—I laid hold of him just as he came off the chapel steps, gave him to Ward, and went after the other two.
JOHN MILLS ( police-constable M 31.) I searched the prisoner, and found five gas-fittings in his trowsers, four cups, a brass rod, and a tumbler in his hat, and a brush—he was rather intoxicated.
EDMUND M'DONALD . I am a policeman. I assisted in searching the prisoner—I found on him 3 s. 3 1/4 d. in copper, and part of the gas-fittings.
Prisoner's Defence. I was made drunk by two men who asked me to carry some gas-fittings and other things to Whitechapel—being drunk, I said I would—they put the things into my trowsers, and told me when they whistled to come out—they were to give me 5 s. or 6 s.
GUILTY .* Aged 20.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18390513-1700]
1700. WILLIAM LUTON was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of May, 2 pairs of shoes, value 10 s., the goods of Joseph Colby.
SUSANNAH COLBY . I am the wife of Joseph Colby, a shoemaker, in Free-school-street. On the morning of the 7th of May, I was in a room behind the shop, and saw the prisoner come in, go behind the counter, take a pair of shoes from the window, and go out—I called out, and my husband ran out after him—I am certain he is the man.
JOSEPH COLBY . I ran into the shop, and saw the prisoner going out—he ran up Fair-street and Potter's-fields, and was taken on Davis-wharf—I let him go when he was brought back, pot missing any thing—I afterwards looked, and missed a pair of shoes—I went out, laid hold of him and gave him in charge—I found a pair of shoes under some dung in Davis-wharf, in the direction he had run.
HENRY ANDREWS . I am a labourer, and live in Fair-street I heard a cry of "Stop thief"—I ran after the prisoner down Potter's-fields, on to Davis-wharf, and stopped him—the dung-heap was in the direction he ran.
PATRICK M'CORMACK ( police-constable M 27.) I took the prisoner and have the shoes.
( Property produced, and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was going to look for work, and being rather behind-hand, I ran—I stopped when I heard the cry—he took me and another boy back to the shop, and let us go—we went away, and he came and took me again.
JOSEPH COLBY re-examined. I took two hoys at first—I had only seen one in the shop, but they were both running together—I never lost sight of the prisoner—I am sure he was in the shop.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Three Months.

1701. SARAH ORGAR was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of May, 1 loaf of bread, value 3 d., the goods of Henry Wallace.
HENRY WALLACE . I am a baker, and live at Putney. The prisoner came to my shop on the 4th of May, about eight o'clock in the morning, took a loaf off the shelf, and concealed it under her shawl—I came into the shop, and she asked me for a two-pound loaf—I weighed it, she paid for it, and walked out with both loaves—I followed her about ten yards, she turned the corner, and I gave her in charge—both loaves were found on her—I knew her before—she lived down a lane close by my shop—she has a husband and a large family.
WILLIAM STOCKS . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner in charge, and found one loaf under her shawl, and one in her hand.
Prisoner. I have six children, and a husband out of employment.
GUILTY . Aged 44.— Confined One Day.

[trialAccount t18390513-1703]
1703. GEORGE LOVETT was indicted for feloniously killing and slaying Mary Lovett.
ISABELLA ATTREE . I am the wife of James Attree, and live in New-street, New Kent-road. The prisoner lived almost opposite me, in the front room down stairs—Mary Castle lived with him, and passed as his wife—she went by the name of Mrs. Lovett—the prisoner is a costermonger, and she went out with cat's meat—on the 14th of March, about half-past five o'clock in the evening, I was standing at my door, at needlework, and saw Mrs. Lovett come up the street with her dog's-meat barrow from her work—I saw her go into her own passage, and directly I heard the prisoner ask a woman if she would meet him at seven o'clock—I am sure it was his voice, I have known him ever since he was a little boy—I cannot tell who he addressed—it was a woman in the house, but I could not hear the woman reply—I heard Mrs. Lovett say to him, "Can't I meet you at seven o'clock? I think I am the fittest person to meet you"—the prisoner made use of very bad expressions, and said, "No"—he ran down stairs—I cannot say whether Mrs. Lovett caught hold of him, but I heard her say to him, "Stop, I want to speak to you"—the prisoner answered, "I don't want to speak to you, nor neither will I be with you"—I then heard a struggle ensue, and in about a minute I heard Mrs. Lovett call out, "Help, help, for God's sake, for he has kicked my eye out;" and then she said, "Oh, help, pray open my eye"—I went opposite the door, and looked into the passage, with other neighbours, and saw her down on the floor, and the prisoner standing over her in the passage down stairs, but not ill-treating her—he was trying to get loose from her, but she was holding him by the coat, and in the struggle to get away from her, he pulled her up on her feet—they struggled till he came to the door, and then he pulled himself out of her band, and went up the street as fast as he could go—I saw no more of him—I saw no blows struck by either of them—when he was gone Mrs. Lovett would have fallen, but I caught her in my arms, took her into the room, and sat her down on a chair—her eye was very much swollen and closed, and the lower part of her face, on the left side, very much bruised—there was a little blood, but that proceeded from her mouth and nose—I washed her face and eye, but it was impossible to open her eye—she appeared perfectly sober, but they were both very much addicted to drinking—he appeared very sober—they were very bad friends, on account of a woman she had taken in out of compassion—I did not attend the deceased further than going and asking how she did—Dean, who came to lodge in the house about a fortnight afterwards, attended her—the surgeon was not called in for some time—I frequently saw her—she kept her bed—she was not able to get up the next day, and she was scarcely able to get up on Saturday—the prisoner did not come back again—I saw him at Union-hall on the Monday when she got a warrant for him, and they went to Union-hall on the Thursday following—I had seen him go down the street in the mean time, but not into the house.
ELIZABETH COOPER . I live opposite the prisoner. I was at my bedroom window on the 14th of March—it looks on the back of the prisoner's premises—about half-past five or six o'clock that evening I looked out, and heard the prisoner say to the woman up stairs, "I will meet yon at seven o'clock"—Mrs. Lovett answered, "Don't meet her, meet me, I am the most right to be met"—the prisoner rushed down stairs, and struck her—she was standing at the back door in the yard—I could hear him fly down stairs—he struck her somewhere about the face, and she fell—I saw him go to kick her—I could see him in the posture going to kick her, and I heard him use a very bad expression, and say, "I will do for you"—she rose herself up by him, as well as she could, and dragged herself into the passage, and I did not see any more—I heard her cry out, "For God's sake, help me"—when I first saw him he was close to her—I saw him strike her—I did not see her strike him—she did not—they were both in a passion—I did not hear her say any thing about another woman—I saw the other woman come out afterwards.
Prisoner, Q. You live behind my house, and two doors from it? A. No, it is across—I can see straight to the yard door—I did not go to Union Hall—I did not think of being a witness till I mentioned it to the beadle.
WILLIAM BARNARD BODDY . I am a surgeon. I was called in to see Mrs. Lovett on the 25th of March, about a fortnight after the transaction—I found her suffering under erysipelas, chiefly about the face and head—the face was very much distended, and swollen dreadfully—she was unable to see from the erysipelas—there was a bruise about the eye—my attention was called to the right eye, but the bruise had then ceased to make any appearance, in consequence of the enlarged state of the head from the erysipelas—I applied all the remedies I conceived to be necessary, poultices, fomentation, and such things, and she became convalescent, I almost thought; but four or five days afterwards another attack of erysipelas came on on the right arm, and then on her left leg, where there had been evidently a very violent blow given—in the left ancle—these symptoms continued increasing, sometimes disappearing and forming again, till at last it brought on an affection of the brain, of which she died—the leg became gangrenous—it brought on an effusion on the brain—there was gangrene, independent of an abscess, just below the tumour formed on the ancle—she died on the 80th of April—I opened the body—the brain was extremely congested and inflamed, and a great quantity of water effused on the brain—that was the immediate cause of death—that was the result of irritation from erysipelas—the erysipelas was no doubt caused by the injuries inflicted—there was severe injury on the face, on one shoulder and on one leg bruises or contusions—I should say all of them together were the cause, none of them in particular causing it—under a constitution like hers, erysipelas would be likely to follow injury—the erysipelas I first discovered was on the face—I saw the injury on the leg at that time, but under all attacks of erysipelas the face is the part first affected—I should think erysipelas would not arise from her drunken habits independent of the injury, but it is possible—I could not tell how long the injury on the leg had existed—I should judge from the appearances that the erysipelas had existed about four or five days, certainly not longer—I think naturally she was a very healthy woman, but from drunken habits her constitution was broken down—I should say erysipelas would be four or five days, or a week, coming on after the injury.
Q. Having heard the injuries were sustained on the 14th of March, can you be certain they were the cause of the erysipelas? A. I have no doubt in my own mind that the erysipelas was the result of the injury, not the least—I speak of the appearances of erysipelas in all parts of the body, all arising from the external injury—when it once begins it travels about from place to place—it very frequently arises without any external injury——drunken habits would no doubt cause it, but there being external injury, I have no doubt that was the cause—had she been more temperate those injuries would have produced any thing but erysipelas—they would have produced a sort of healthy inflammation.
Prisoner. The wound in her leg she had had four or five years.
Witness. I should not think it was a very recent wound—it was muchl onger than a fortnight—I should think it was not so recent as the wound on the head—the one on the ancle was the most serious part of the injury—it had penetrated through the whole of the joint, which must have produced very great irritation in the system—erysipelas would be most likely to attack the part which had been previously injured—that wound itself would not have produced erysipelas, it must have been the result of more recent injury—the erysipelas first commenced in the head—erysipelas in the leg did not make its appearance for two or three weeks after I saw her—the wound in the leg could not have caused the erysipelas in the face.
ISABELLA ATTREE re-examined. The 14th of March was Thursday—I, got hot water and washed her face, and put her to bed—I saw her next morning, and she complained of her head, and of being very ill indeed—I saw her every day till her death—her face was in a most dreadful state—I did not observe that for about a fortnight after she received the blow, but it was always swollen from the time she received the blow—I had seen her before she received the blow, three or four times a day—I saw her that morning before she went out, there was nothing the matter with her face then—there was no breaking out, or any humour at all—she was about forty yean old—I had known her fourteen years—I never saw her in liquor after this—I do not think she took more than half a pint of beer a day—she was never able to go about her business afterwards—I never saw the prisoner at the house afterwards—I have seen him go down the street—she expressed a wish to see him, but he sent word he would not come—I know the situation of Cooper's bed-room, window—I have never been in the room—it faces right over the back-yard—I should say a person could see the back-door of Lovett's house very well from that window—I cannot see the window from the passage—I dare say I could from the back door—I was standing at my own door at first—I then went across, not into the house, but on the pavement opposite the door, which was open—I looked through the passage, which is a straight one—what I saw was more in the middle of the passage—the backdoor was open, and comes to the foot of the stairs—she had struggled, apparently, into the passage—but he was then trying to get from her, and dragged her to the front-door.
ELIZABETH COOPER re-examined. I am quite sure I could see the back-door from my window—I could not see into the house—what I saw took place just at the step of the back-yard, not in the passage—I went down stairs afterwards, she was then leaning against the window-ledge.
Prisoner's Defence ( written.) "As I was coming down stairs the deceased came out of the front-room, seized me by the throat, and dragged
me off four stairs—kicked me twice in ***—I then struck her in the face—she took out a warrant, and I applied at Union-hall—the Magistrate ordered the warrant to stand over for three months, since which I have not seen her—witnesses can prove the state she went to their house in on the 21st."
WILLIAM CARTER . I lived near the prisoner—on the 21st of March I saw Mrs. Lovett about that time, at our house, between seven and eight o'clock in the evening—she was quite sober—I saw her face—her eye, appeared to be a little swollen.
ELIZABETH DEAN . I am the wife of George Dean, and live in New-street. I have been there six weeks—I went first on Tuesday, five weeks before Mrs. Lovett died—I attended on her, and nursed her all the time—she was not able to go about her business—she was confined to her bed all the time, except being lifted in and out, and that with great difficulty—Mr. Boddy was attending her the day I first went to the house—I can tell no more than him.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390513-1704]
1704. CHARLES RYAN, alias Charles Callaghan, was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Nathan Smith, about the hour of 1 in the night, on the 24th of April, with intent to steal, and stealing therein, 1 looking-glass, value 10 s., his property.
NATHAN SMITH . I live in Bridge-street, in the parish of Bermondsey. On Wednesday, the 24th of April, I went to bed about half-past ten o'clock—I was the last person up—I thought all the doors and windows were fast—I had been out all day, and came in at that time—I did not examine them—I saw that the back-door was fast, and went up to bed directly—I fastened the front-door myself—I got up just before eight o'clock in the morning, and went out directly—I saw the back-door was open, but went out without taking further notice, thinking some of my daughters had been down and opened it.
CAROLINE SMITH . I am the prosecutor's daughter. I went to bed on Wednesday, the 24th of April, at ten o'clock, before my father came home—I bolted the back-door—I got up just before eight o'clock in the morning, after my father—this looking-glass was on the sideboard the night before, I am sure—I missed it in about ten minutes after I came down—I found the back-door open—the bolts were broken—the back-room door was broken open also, which was locked when I went to bed—the glass had been in that room.
JAMES BRASNAN . I am a policeman. I was on duty at Bermondsey on Thursday morning the 25th or April, and saw the prisoner about half-past one o'clock coining from the back of a house in George-street with a looking-glass frame—I stopped him and asked him where he got it—he said at the back of Russell-street, which is about three hundred yards from Smith's house—I took it into my hand, and he ran off—I followed and secured him—I am sure he is the man—T had lost sight of him at a short turning, but I know his person—I saw his face at first.
WILLIAM MILLER . I am a policeman. On Thursday, the 25th of April, I was on duty in Bermondsey—I picked up a glass in White Bear-yard about three hundred yards from where Brasnan stopped the prisoner and about six hundred yards from the prosecutor's.
NATHAN SMITH re-examined. This is the frame of my looking-glass—I have had it twenty-four or twenty-five years—I have not the least doubt of it—this glass belongs to the frame.
Prisoner's Defence. As I was coming along a lad named Mike Coleman called me—be is always living in the prosecutor's house—he asked me to carry that frame for him—I said yes, and coming into George-street he went another way—he was at the house the same night before the girls went to bed—he was always there along with them, and he had a key of the door—he got in and took it—he told me so—that he had a key of the back-door and the room-door, and there were no bolts on the door at all.
NATHAN SMITH re-examined. I know the lad be names—I never saw him at that house—I had lived there about three months—I have no doubt that lad was along with the prisoner—I had met him one day about three weeks previous to the robbery, and have not seen him since—I had employed him when I kept a broker's shop in Bermondsey-street, but heard he was a bad character and parted with him—I know nothing of the prisoner.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined One Year.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18390513-1705]
1705. JAMES CLARKE and RICHARD JACKSON were indicted for stealing, on the 16th of May, 1 purse, value 1 d.;2 sovereigns, 4 half-crowns, 10 shillings, and 4 1/2 pence in copper, the goods and monies of Sarah Emery, from her person.
MR. PAYNE conducted the prosecution.
SARAH EMERY . I live in Wellington-street, Newington-causeway. On Wednesday last I went to Mitcham to see a friend—it was the day of Dorking-races—I left Mitcham between eleven and twelve o'clock at night, and was the worse for liquor—I went into an inn at Mitcham—I had three sovereigns in my purse when I went into the house—I had received them from my friend—I could not find a coach at the inn—I waited there about a quarter of an hour, and had a glass of brandy-and-water—I changed a sovereign to pay for it, and received 19 s. in change, which consisted of half-crowns and shillings—I put them into the other end of my purse—I also had one shilling in my pocket, and 4 1/2 d. in copper—not finding a coach, I walked on towards Tooting, and between Mitcham and Tooting I heard a glass coach come by—I then took my purse out of my pocket and put it into my stocking—when the coach came up I asked the man if he could convey me to London—he said yes—it was the prisoner Clarke—he got down and let me inside the coach—there was somebody rather shorter than himself on the box, who I cannot recollect—I found a little girl and boy inside—the little girl was very anxious indeed to get home—after getting a little way the horse would not go on—we got out and walked a little way, as Clarke asked me—I walked a very little way, being very ill and very sick, I was obliged to get in again, and the children got in again soon alter—soon after a man came and took the children away—they said they must have some other conveyance to get them home—I do not know where this was—they left me in the coach, and I went to sleep—I was awoke by Clarke asking me if I was cold—I said, yes, I was very cold—he covered me over with a great coat—I do not think he stopped long inside, but I slept so soundly I do not know—I went to sleep again directly—I cannot say when I awoke again, but I was sick and vomiting, and he opened the coach-door—at Vauxhall-bridge he came to the door, and told me I must get out, which I did, and asked him where I was—he said, "At Vauxhall-bridge"—he wished me good morning—he did not ask me for any money for the ride—I went on
two or three steps, and saw a policeman—I asked him where I could have a cab or coach to take me home—he said, "At the Horns, or Kennington-cross"—I instantly put my hand down to my pocket, and missed my money, and said, "Oh God, I am robbed"—when I got out of the coach I recollect seeing the other prisoner on the box—I found my stocking either cut or torn—they were new stockings, and were not so when I got into the coach—I am quite sure my purse was in the stocking when I got into the coach—it was my right stocking—I had boots on, and it laid just on the top of the boot—the policeman and I went to a stable at Westminster, and found the two prisoners there, and he took them into custody—I have never found my purse—it was daylight when I was at Vauxhall-bridge—we were four hours coming from Tooting there, as one of the horses would not go—they were obliged to push the vehicle along—they had come from Epsom—the shilling and copper were gone from my pocket, as well as the purse from my stocking.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You had passed the day at Mitcham, had you? A. No—I cannot say what time I got down there—I was sober when I got there—I left town by the coach about two, or three, or four o'clock—I cannot say what time—I do not know how far Mitcham is from London—I never went there before—I went to meet my friend there—it was a gentleman—I never mention any friend's name—I met him at a friend's house there—it does not signify to yon whose house—he took me to a place—I do not know the place—I went to have something to drink there—it was a public-house—I call that a friend's house—I do not know the sign—I was to meet him not far from the cricket-ground, and did so—I knew the cricket-ground, at I was there once before, and saw them play at cricket—I did not say I had never been to Mitcham before—I have been there once, but never but once—that was about a week before—I was not with a friend then—I met my friend this time at the first inn, opposite the cricket-ground, on the right-hand side—I had seen him in town two days before, and promised to meet him at Mitcham—he mentioned the first public-house opposite the cricket-ground—it was there I got a little drink—I did not dine there—I dined at home—I was very much in liquor, very sick, and very ill—I am a gay woman, that is the truth—I cannot recollect any body but Clarke coming in the coach with me—he awoke me, asked if I was cold, and covered me over—whether he came in more than once, or whether any body else came in, I do not know—he might come more than once, and not awake me—I am not aware that I said when I was taken up at Tooting, that I had no money, and could not get home, and wanted a ride—I will not swear I did dot say so—I made no agreement with him—I only saw two persons on the coach when they took me up, one tall and one shorter—the shorter one is not here, I suppose, but I do not know—Clarke is the only one I know—I saw Jackson when I got off the coach-box—he was not the person on the box when I got in—I never saw him till I got out.
COURT. Q. Then, was the person sitting with Clarke, a different person to Jackson? A. I did not notice his face—I do not know whether he is the second man or not—my foot was not cut or scratched.
Jackson. I took the two children out, while the coachman went inside to the lady—I walked up and down with them while Clarke went to look for a horse and chaise to take the children home—don't you recollect my being in the coach? Witness. No, I do not.
WILLIAM HAYNES ( police-constable L 69.) I was on duty in Vauxhall,
on Thursday morning at half-past four o'clock, near the bridge—I saw a glass-coach stop at Vauxhall-gate—both the prisoners were on the box—Clarke got down and let the prosecutrix out—she crossed the road from the gate towards High-street—the coach went over Vauxhall-bridge-about three or four minutes after it was gone, the prosecutrix came and asked if I could tell her where there was a cabstand—she complained of being robbed, and described what she had lost—I went in search of the coach, and traced it to the Ship-yard, Horseferry-road—the prosecutrix followed me—I arrived there first with a brother-constable—I found both the prisoners there and the coach—I took them into custody—when the prosecutrix came up she told Clarke he was the party she suspected had robbed her, that he was the only one who had got into the coach with her—he said he knew nothing at all about it—I took them to the station-house, and on Clarke found one sovereign, a half-crown and two shillings in his breast-pocket, and fivepence halfpenny in copper in his outside coat pocket—I found on Jackson one sovereign and 1 l. 2 s. 1/2 d., all together loose in his right-hand jacket pocket—there are five half-crowns, seven shillings, four sixpences, and a fourpenny-piece, one penny-piece and three halfpence—he said he got it by strapping horses on the Derby-day at Epsom, and he gave silver for the sovereign at a booth at Epsom—I asked him where the booth was—he said he did not know what booth it was, nor where it was—Clarke claimed the money he had—the prosecutrix showed me her stocking—I have it here—it appears to have been either torn or cut just above where her boot would come, on the right leg—she told me before any thing was found, that she had lost two sovereigns and 19 s. in her purse, and fourpence halfpenny and one shilling out of her pocket—I asked Clarke id he knew Jackson—he said he had know him for years by being about the Marsh-gate.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you mention that before the Magistrate? A. I did—my deposition was read over to me and I signed it.
MR. BALLANTINE called
WILLIAM FORD . I am a hackney-coach driver. On the day in question I drove a glass-coach for Mr.Ward, my master—Clarke merely had a ride with me down to Epsom—he is a servant of Mr. Ward's—coming back, one was a job-horse—when we got to Tooting we gave two gentlemen a ride, but the horse gibbed, and we stopped at the Castle at Tooting, and the gentlemen got out—the party we took down to Epsom had come back in another conveyance—I saw the prosecutrix about a hundred yards from the Castle at Tooting—we were on the off-side of the road, trying to get the horse on—she came up and said, "If you will let get on the box, I will get them along"—she said, "Will you give me a ride to town? I have been to Epsom and have lost all my money, and lost my friends"—I said, "I can't give you a ride to town, I can't give ourselves one I am going to walk"—I meant I could not get the horses along—I had two children with me, one my master's and one belonging to a person named Bonner—one of the ladies who went down with me was to ride back in a gig, and I was to take the two children back—the prosecutrix at last got into the coach—Jackson was at that time standing about there, and he agreed to help us back—he was not in the coach—I said, "will take the two children and walk, we cannot get them on"—there were two policemen there, also a man who said he was a knacker, and a man in a blue jacket; who
helped me along—I left the coach, and walked with the children to Clapham, where we got a cab —I am quite sure of what she said about the money.
MR. CHAMBERS. Q. Do you know both these men? A. I know Jackson by sight—he never comes to our stable—I have seen him about the Marsh-gate—Clarke was in my master's employ as a cab-driver—he generally stands at Palace-yard—the prosecutrix got into the coach herself—I shut the door after her—the children rode with her as far as they went—I left the prisoners at Tooting—I told them I would endeavour to get another horse to help the carriage on—I left the man in the blue jacket with them.
COURT. A. There was no opportunity for any body to rob her while you were there? A. No, there were two policemen there at the time—I was in the coach with her after it moved on—the children had got out then, and she allowed me to take liberties with her—she, got into the coach a second time for that purpose—she was perfectly aware what she was about—she said, "Mind you don't break my bonnet"—she was intoxicated—she was sick on the pavement when she first came up—before she got into the coach and afterwards—she was never asleep in my presence.
SARAH EMERY re-examined. I have heard what this witness has stated—I have no recollection of it at all—he must have awoke me if it was so—I am quite sure it did not occur—he had nothing to do with me—I don't recollect that he was in the coach with me.
HENRY WARD . Clarke was in my employment five months as a cabdriver—I knew him a long time before that—his character was very good indeed—I employed Ford to take this coach to Epsom on this day—Clarke asked leave to ride behind, and I said he might—Clarke was not to receive any money for the job himself, but he ought to have had two days' earnings of mine about him—he ought to have given me 1 l., free of his own earnings, for two nights—he had not paid me any money the morning before—I often trust him two or three nights.
WILLIAM FORD re-examined. I left the coach at Tooting—it did not move at all after it moved the first hundred yards, while I was there—I only went a hundred yards with it while she was in it—I left her in it—the two prisoners and the man in the blue jacket were with the coach when I left—that man is not here—I do not know him—he was not on the coach—he was helping to start the horses—he had some supper with us at the Castle—we stopped at the Castle an hour—I staid there about half an hour after she got in—the policeman went away—she sat with her nice to the horses.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390513-1706]
1706. JAMES MATTOCKS was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of April, 1 opera-glass, value 10 s., of William Smeeton, his muster.
WILLIAM SMEETON . I am a poulterer, and live in Bermondsey-street The prisoner was in my employ, and lived six months in the house—on the 12th of April, about a quarter past seven o'clock in the morning I was in my bed-room—I heard the prisoner calling "Master"—I went down and found him on the floor in the passage, apparently in hysterics—I moved him into the sitting-room—he remained in the fit some time, till I talked of sending for a medical man—then the fit appeared to leave him, and I asked him what had been amiss—he said the place had been robbed, and he had been knocked down by one or two men, and one man he believed came from the cupboard, and the other he did not know where he came from—I
found twelve silver spoons removed from the sitting-room and lying in the cupboard, under the stair-case, on a candle-box—I missed a metal watch from the drawer of my desk, two five-shilling papers of halfpence, an opera-glass and a musical snuff-box—a case of stuffed birds were moved from the mantel-piece and was lying on the floor—two squares of glass in the window were broken in the sitting room even with the shop—the shutter was taken down but not at all broken—there was no external violence—a Venetian blind was broken in pieces on the floor—the home was safe when I went to bed over night—I found a screw-driver in the shop that morning, it corresponded with the marks on my desk where the money and watch were taken from—I did not suspect the prisoner—he remained in my service till last Monday—I have a key which opens the drawer under my desk—the policeman produced to me a duplicate key which I kept in a small drawer in the top of my desk, and I missed it from that drawer—I had a good deal of bad money which I had taken for years past, and I missed some of that from a bag.
JAMES HATWARD . I am a pawnbroker. I produce an opera glass pawned on the 6th of April, for 3 s., in the name of John Maddocks—it was a butcher-boy, and I believe it was the prisoner—here is the counterpart of my duplicate.
THOMAS JAMES CAVANNAH . I am shopman to Mr. Cole, pawnbroker, Waterloo-road, Lambeth. I produce a metal watch pawned on the 6th of April, in the name of James Maddocks, by a butcher-boy, who I believe to be the prisoner, from his dress, size, and complexion—I have no recollection of his features—here is the counterpart of my duplicate.
JOHN FALLIS ( police-constable M 122.) I took the prisoner on Monday, the 13th of May, and found six duplicates on him, one for the watch, one for the opera-glass—I found four keys, a ring, and one half-crown, five shillings, and one sixpence, all bad money—one of the keys unlocked the drawers—I compared the screw-driver with the marks on the desk—they appeared to have been done by that.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18390513-1707]
1707. WILLIAM RICHARDS was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of May, 1 1/4 lb. weight of tobacco, value 5 s., the goods of Charles Davies, his master.
JAMES STEELE . I am in the employ of Mr. Charles Davies, a tobacco manufacturer, of Coles-street, Dover-road. The prisoner was in the employ of Mr. Farebrother, who is in Mr. Davies' service—it is Mr. Davies practice to rub persons down as they leave work—on the evening of the 7th of May, on rubbing down the prisoner he discovered a piece of cigar—he then ordered me to search him, and I took this roll of tobacco from him—it was concealed in his trowsers—here is 1 1/4 lb. weight—it is worth 5 s.
WILLIAM STOTTER ( police-constable M 136.) I took the prisoner—I found a quantity of loose leaf tobacco in his cap—this is it.
JAMES STEEL . We had tobacco like this in the loft.
Prisoner. I did not have it at all.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 15.— Confined One Week, and Whipped.
[trialAccount t18390513-1708]
1708. JOHN WILSON was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of May, 1 1/4 lb. weight of tobacco, value 5 s., the goods of Charles Davies.
JAMES STEEL . I am in the service of Mr. Charles Davies. The prisoner was in his employ, and was searched on the 7th of May, I found on him this roll of tobacco, which he had no right to—he said it was the first time he had done so.
WILLIAM STOTTER . I took this prisoner—I found a quantity of leaf tobacco in his cap also—while I was searching one boy's cap, the other took off his cap, and threw the tobacco from his cap amongst the other, so I was forced to mix it.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 15,— Confined One Week, and Whipped.

1709. MARY BAYNON was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of April, 1 pewter pot, value 1 s., the goods of John Thomas Percy; 1 pewter pot, value 1 s., the goods of William Nelson; 2 pewter pots, value 2 s., the goods of Ann Willmot; 1 pewter pot, value 1 s. 8 d., the goods of Thomas Davey; and 1 pewter pot, value 1 s. 8 d., the goods of Joseph Towers: and FRANCES BAYNON , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MR. JERNINGHAM conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN THOMAS PERCY . I am a publican, and live at Rotherhithe. Here is a pewter pot of mine—it has my name and address on it.
CHARLES HALLAN ( police-sergeant R 3.) I went to the prisoner's premises—I found Frances Baynon, the mother, up in a comer of the top-room, and there were two pint pots by the side of her—she was hustling amongst shavings—she said she told her daughter not to bring them—one of these pots is Mr. Percy's.
FRANCES BAYNON . I was cleaning down stain—he asked where I lived, and I told him up stairs—he said, "Go tip, and I will follow you."
Witness. That is wrong—I asked a little girl down stairs, and I found yon in the corner of the room—I then searched the shavings and found these two quart and two pint pots besides.
THOMAS DAVEY . I am a publican. One of these quart pots is mine, it has my name and address on it.
WILLIAM NELSON . One of these pots is mine.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390513-1710]
1710. MARY BAYNON was again indicted for stealing, on the 15th of April, 1 pewter pot, value 1 s., the goods of Thomas Lulham.
THOMAS LULHAM . I keep the Neptune public-house, in Neptune-street, Rotherhithe. This pint pot is mine—it has my name and address on it—on Monday the 15th of April, I was watching, and saw the prisoner—I followed her down York-street—I there lost sight of her—I then came back to Adam-street, and was talking with a person—I saw the prisoner again, and watched her—she crossed over—I saw her stoop against a door, With a pretence of tying her shoe—she took up something, and put it under her arm—I pulled her shawlaside, and I found this pot of mine under her arm—she said she hoped I would forgive her as it was the first time.
(The prisoner pleaded poverty.)
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Recorder,
[trialAccount t18390513-1711]
1711. JOHN WEST was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MESSRS. CHAMBERS and SHEA conducted the Prosecution.
EMMA JEMIMA SMITH . I am the wife of William Henry Smith, a green-grocer
in the Borough. On the 30th of March, about nine o'clock at night, the prisoner came to my shop for a halfpennyworth of water-cresses and a halfpennyworth of pot herbs, and he told me to send half a cwt of coals to Lant-street—he gave me a half-crown to pay for what he bad bought, which came to 1 d.—I had not got change, and took the half-crown next door to Mr. Holloway's, who came back with me, and told the prisoner it was bad—he said his father had taken it for wages, and he lived in Lant-place (he did not say the number) they left my shop to go there.
JAMES HOLLOWAY . I am a grocer. Mrs. Smith brought me a half-crown—I went back to her shop, and told the prisoner it was bad, and asked where he got it—he said his father had taken it in his wages, and had sent him for the cresses and pot herbs, that his father lived in Lant-place—I went there with him, and said to him, "This is Lant-place"—he said, "Yes, but I am d—d if I'll go"—he ran down George-street—I kept the half-crown, and gave it to the policeman.
WILLIAM HUMPHREYS ( police-constable M 127.) I was on duty in Fryer-street—I heard a cry of "Stop thief," and saw the prisoner running—I took him, and Mr. Holloway came up—I received this half-crown from him.
SARAH MANNERS . My husband keeps the Feathers, in Storey-street, Borough-market. On the 8th of April, the prisoner came for a penny biscuit—I served him—he gave me a bad sixpence—I said, "This is a bad one"—he said, "Is it?"—I marked it, and gave it to the policeman.
JAMES GARVAN ( police-constable M 44.) I was called by Mrs. Manners on the 8th of April—she gave me this sixpence—I took the prisoner into her parlour—I found on him a good sixpence, a penny-piece, two half-pence, and a duplicate of a handkerchief pawned for 6 d., which he said be got the sixpence for.
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am Inspector of coin to the Royal Mint. These are both counterfeit.
GUILTY . Aged 15.— Confined Twelve Months.?
[trialAccount t18390513-1712]
1712. HENRY HARRISON was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of April, 5 veneers, value 15 s., the goods of William Fear, his master.
WILLIAM FEAR . I am a cabinet-maker, and live in Long-lane, Bermondsey—my workshop is in the rear of my house. The prisoner was a turner in my employ, and had the keys to open my premises, at five o'clock in the morning—on the 29th of April I missed about fifteen rosewood veneers—I sent for the prisoner, told him he was aware I had been robbed for a length of time, and my suspicions fell on him—he denied it—I afterwards got a constable, and went to the prisoner's lodging, in Baalzephon-street—the prisoner was there—I then mentioned to him the name of Mr. Tilley, who had charged him with taking some veneers, and I desired the prisoner to come to my parlour, that he might see Tilley—he came and saw him, and Tilley charged him with taking six veneers—the prisoner said, "Tilley, don't be so hard, it is only five I have taken"—I then begged the prisoner to tell who his accomplices were—he denied, with great violence, that he had any accomplices.
WILLIAM TILLEY . I am a turner. On the 27th of April I lived in a street facing the back of Mr. Fear's premises—early that morning I heard a noise—I got up, and looked out of the window—I saw some veneers, falling to the ground—one of them had alighted on a little
out-house, and the rest were falling—they had been thrown over a wall—I watched, and saw the prisoner come from that direction, and go up a garden belonging to a Mr. Smith—he gathered them up, and then looked about—he saw me and my wife at the window—he dropped them, and went out of the garden—I then drew down the blinds of my window, and watched—he came again, looked up, and saw no one—he then gathered up the veneers, and put them over a wall leading to his own lodging.
WILLIAM SHIELDS ( police-constable M 157.) I went on the 29th of April to the prisoner's lodgings, with Mr. Fear, and brought the prisoner to his house—the prosecutor said, "Now, Harrison, here are you and Mr. Tilley, face to face; I want to know who is the receiver?"—the prisoner said, "I really cannot tell you; what I took I took myself "—I took the prisoner to the station—his lodging was not searched.
Prisoner's Defence. I am innocent—I believe there is a prejudice between the witness and me—he wrote to my master at one time, and asked to come back to work, and then I was discharged—I was then sent for again, and he left altogether.
WILLIAM TILLEY . I work in quite a distinct branch to him—I have no prejudice against him.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Transported for Seven Years.

1713. ANN ARCHER was indicted for concealing the birth of a child.
NOT GUILTY .
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, 17 TH OF JUNE.
